Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Incoherence or Word Salad: Severe fragmentation of speech and sentence structure, making it nearly impossible to understand.\n  - Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  - Childlike Silliness: Inappropriate, childish, or illogical behavior appearing in situations.\n  - Stupor: No psychomotor activity; not actively relating to environment.\n  - Catalepsy: Passive induction of a posture held against gravity.\n  - Waxy Flexibility: Slight, even resistance to positioning by examiner.\n  - Avolition: A decrease in motivated self-initiated purposeful activities.\n  - Alogia: A state where the quantity of spontaneous speech is significantly reduced.\n  - Anhedonia: A decrease in the ability to experience pleasure or interest in enjoyable activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Initial Insomnia: Difficulty falling asleep and taking a long time to do so.\n  - Middle Insomnia: Waking up frequently during sleep or having difficulty falling back asleep.\n  - Early Morning Awakening: Waking up too early and being unable to fall back asleep.\n  - Hypersomnia: Sleeping for long periods at night or excessive sleep during the day.\n  - Nightmare Disorder: Sleep disturbance caused by repeated and distressing dreams.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n  - Derailment or Loose Associations: A speech pattern of switching rapidly from one topic to another with no logical connection between thoughts.\n  - Tangentiality: Speech that deviates entirely from the question and fails to answer directly.\n  - Incoherence or Word Salad: Severe fragmentation of speech and sentence structure, making it nearly impossible to understand.\n  - Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  - Childlike Silliness: Inappropriate, childish, or illogical behavior appearing in situations.\n  - Stupor: No psychomotor activity; not actively relating to environment.\n  - Catalepsy: Passive induction of a posture held against gravity.\n  - Waxy Flexibility: Slight, even resistance to positioning by examiner.\n  - Mutism: No, or very little, verbal response despite the ability to speak.\n  - Negativism: Opposition or no response to instructions or external stimuli without apparent motive.\n  - Posturing: Spontaneous and active maintenance of a posture against gravity.\n  - Mannerism: Odd, circumstantial caricature of normal actions.\n  - Stereotypy: Repetitive, abnormally frequent, non-goal-directed movements.\n  - Agitation: Excessive motor activity without obvious external cause.\n  - Grimacing: Contorted facial expressions irrelevant to the situation.\n  - Echolalia: Mimicking another's speech.\n  - Echopraxia: Mimicking another's movements.\n  - Diminished Emotional Expression: A state of reduced emotional expression in facial expressions, eye contact, intonation of speech, and movements.\n  - Avolition: A decrease in motivated self-initiated purposeful activities.\n  - Alogia: A state where the quantity of spontaneous speech is significantly reduced.\n  - Anhedonia: A decrease in the ability to experience pleasure or interest in enjoyable activities.\n  - Asociality: A state where interest in social activities or interpersonal relationships is significantly reduced.\n  Duration: 8 months\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Initial Insomnia: Difficulty falling asleep and taking a long time to do so.\n  - Middle Insomnia: Waking up frequently during sleep or having difficulty falling back asleep.\n  - Early Morning Awakening: Waking up too early and being unable to fall back asleep.\n  - Hypersomnia: Sleeping for long periods at night or excessive sleep during the day.\n  - Nightmare Disorder: Sleep disturbance caused by repeated and distressing dreams.\n  - Non-Restorative Sleep: Feeling unrefreshed despite having slept enough.\n  Duration: 6 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - {"candidates": ["Schizophrenia", "Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type)", "Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features"], "note": "The patient's description of being constantly watched, hearing voices, and experiencing deep depression aligns with the previous candidates. The key detail that these experiences, including auditory hallucinations, can occur independently of depressive episodes slightly reduces the likelihood of Delusional Disorder, as it typically involves persistent delusions without the full symptom complex of schizophrenia. However, the presence of both depressive symptoms and psychotic features (delusions of being watched and auditory hallucinations) suggests that Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type) and Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features remain strong candidates. Schizophrenia is still a consideration due to the nature and duration of the psychotic symptoms. Delusional Disorder is less likely due to the presence of auditory hallucinations and significant depressive symptoms, so it has been removed from the list. Further evaluation is needed to differentiate between these diagnoses, especially to assess the timing and relationship between depressive and psychotic symptoms.", "is_final": false}

No matter where I go, I can feel their eyes on me, and it makes me feel incredibly unsafe and anxious in my own home. I know it sounds hard to believe, but I am certain they are targeting me, and the threat is completely real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of being watched, and have you noticed any relationship between these episodes and your depressive symptoms, such as whether one seems to trigger or worsen the other?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling watched for about eight months during this current stretch, though my overall struggles began about two and a half years ago. In fact, I've had periods of at least two weeks where I felt watched and heard things even when I wasn't feeling depressed at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing things, can you describe what you hear during these episodes, and how often these auditory experiences occur in relation to your feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: I usually hear low whispering or voices talking about me in the background, which makes the feeling of being watched so much more intense and terrifying. These voices and the paranoia almost always happen together, making me feel completely unsafe, though there are still times I feel watched even when everything is quiet.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of the total duration of your mental health struggles, about two and a half years, has been dominated by depressive symptoms versus these psychotic experiences, and how have these symptoms impacted your daily functioning and relationships?"

The depression has definitely been there for the vast majority of these two years and five months, even when the paranoia and voices aren't as active. Mutism: No, or very little, verbal response despite the ability to speak.\n  - Negativism: Opposition or no response to instructions or external stimuli without apparent motive.\n  - Posturing: Spontaneous and active maintenance of a posture against gravity.\n  - Mannerism: Odd, circumstantial caricature of normal actions.\n  - Stereotypy: Repetitive, abnormally frequent, non-goal-directed movements.\n  - Agitation: Excessive motor activity without obvious external cause.\n  - Grimacing: Contorted facial expressions irrelevant to the situation.\n  - Echolalia: Mimicking another's speech.\n  - Echopraxia: Mimicking another's movements.\n  - Diminished Emotional Expression: A state of reduced emotional expression in facial expressions, eye contact, intonation of speech, and movements.\n  - Avolition: A decrease in motivated self-initiated purposeful activities.\n  - Alogia: A state where the quantity of spontaneous speech is significantly reduced.\n  - Anhedonia: A decrease in the ability to experience pleasure or interest in enjoyable activities.\n  - Asociality: A state where interest in social activities or interpersonal relationships is significantly reduced.\n  Duration: 8 months\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Initial Insomnia: Difficulty falling asleep and taking a long time to do so.\n  - Middle Insomnia: Waking up frequently during sleep or having difficulty falling back asleep.\n  - Early Morning Awakening: Waking up too early and being unable to fall back asleep.\n  - Hypersomnia: Sleeping for long periods at night or excessive sleep during the day.\n  - Nightmare Disorder: Sleep disturbance caused by repeated and distressing dreams.\n  - Non-Restorative Sleep: Feeling unrefreshed despite having slept enough.\n  Duration: 6 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 2 years and 5 months\n  - Additional: psychotic_symptoms and depressive_episode occur concurrently\n  - Additional: depressive_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness (Criterion C)\n  - Additional: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode (Criterion B)\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: Delusions\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(No match in profile or inference only)\nAnswer strategy: {\"matched\": true, \"matched_sections\": [\"Critical Diagnostic Criteria\", \"psychotic_symptoms\", \"depressive_episode\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"Explain that you haven't"

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here today because I've been struggling with severe mental health issues for about two years and five months now. It has been a really difficult mix of deep depression and some very unusual, distressing experiences that make me feel paranoid and unsafe. I just really need some help because it has been going on for so long and I can't cope anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these unusual and distressing experiences that make you feel paranoid and unsafe, what exactly happens during these episodes?\n\nPatient: It feels like I am constantly being watched and monitored by a specific organization, and they are tracking my every move. I know it sounds hard to believe, but I am certain they are targeting me, and the threat is completely real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of being watched, and have you noticed any relationship between these episodes and your depressive symptoms, such as whether one seems to trigger or worsen the other?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling watched for about eight months during this current stretch, though my overall struggles began about two and a half years ago. While my depression and these paranoid feelings often happen at the same time, they don't always trigger each other. In fact, I've had periods of at least two weeks where I felt watched and heard things even when I wasn't feeling depressed at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing things, can you describe what you hear during these episodes, and how often these auditory experiences occur in relation to your feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: I usually hear low whispering or voices talking about me in the background, which makes the feeling of being watched so much more intense and terrifying. These voices and the paranoia almost always happen together, making me feel completely unsafe, though there are still times I feel watched even when everything is quiet.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of the total duration of your mental health struggles, about two and a half years, has been dominated by depressive symptoms versus these psychotic experiences, and how have these symptoms impacted your daily functioning and relationships?\n\nPatient: The depression has definitely been there for the vast majority of these two years and five months, even when the paranoia and voices aren't as active. It has completely ruined my daily life and relationships because I've lost all my motivation to do basic tasks and have entirely withdrawn from everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods where you experienced significant depressive symptoms without any psychotic symptoms, such as the feelings of being watched or hearing voices, and if so, how long did those periods last?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. For the first year and a half of my struggles, I only had the severe depression without any of the paranoia or voices. Those scary experiences didn't start until about eight months ago.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your depressive symptoms changed, if at all, when the psychotic symptoms, such as the feelings of being watched and hearing voices, first appeared about eight months ago?"

Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n  - Early Morning Awakening: Waking up too early and being unable to fall back asleep.\n  - Hypersomnia: Sleeping for long periods at night or excessive sleep during the day.\n  - Nightmare Disorder: Sleep disturbance caused by repeated and distressing dreams.\n  - Do not use any diagnosis names outside of this list:\n- Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Combined Presentation)\n- Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Inattentive Presentation)\n- Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder (Predominantly Hyperactive/Impulsive Presentation)\n- Delusional Disorder\n- Schizophrenia\n- Schizoaffective Disorder (Bipolar Type)\n- Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type)\n- Bipolar I Disorder\n- Bipolar II Disorder\n- Bipolar I Disorder with Psychotic Features\n- Generalized Anxiety Disorder\n- Specific Phobia\n- Major Depressive Disorder\n- Persistent Depressive Disorder\n- Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features\n- Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder\n- Body Dysmorphic Disorder\n- Posttraumatic Stress Disorder\n- Acute Stress Disorder\n- Adjustment Disorder\n- Anorexia Nervosa\n- Bulimia Nervosa\n- Binge-Eating Disorder\n\n[Important — Note Field Formatting]\nIn the \"note\" field, you MUST explain WHY the candidate list has changed or remained the same\ncompared to the previous candidates.\nFor example: \"Previously the candidates were [A, B, C], but based on this conversation,\nC seems unlikely because [reason], so the current candidates are [A, B].\"\n\n[When to End the Interview]\nSet \"is_final\": true ONLY when you have gathered sufficient evidence to make a confident\nfinal diagnosis and no further questioning is needed.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Interview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you please tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here today because I've been struggling with severe mental health issues for about two years and five months now. It has been a really difficult mix of deep depression and some very unusual, distressing experiences that make me feel paranoid and unsafe. I just really need some help because it has been going on for so long and I can't cope anymore.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about these unusual and distressing experiences that make you feel paranoid and unsafe, what exactly happens during these episodes?\n\nPatient: It feels like I am constantly being watched and monitored by a specific organization, and they are tracking my every move. No matter where I go, I can feel their eyes on me, and it makes me feel incredibly unsafe and anxious in my own home. I know it sounds hard to believe, but I am certain they are targeting me, and the threat is completely real.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing these feelings of being watched, and have you noticed any relationship between these episodes and your depressive symptoms, such as whether one seems to trigger or worsen the other?\n\nPatient: I've been feeling watched for about eight months during this current stretch, though my overall struggles began about two and a half years ago. While my depression and these paranoid feelings often happen at the same time, they don't always trigger each other. In fact, I've had periods of at least two weeks where I felt watched and heard things even when I wasn't feeling depressed at all.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned hearing things, can you describe what you hear during these episodes, and how often these auditory experiences occur in relation to your feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: I usually hear low whispering or voices talking about me in the background, which makes the feeling of being watched so much more intense and terrifying. These voices and the paranoia almost always happen together, making me feel completely unsafe, though there are still times I feel watched even when everything is quiet.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how much of the total duration of your mental health struggles, about two and a half years, has been dominated by depressive symptoms versus these psychotic experiences, and how have these symptoms impacted your daily functioning and relationships?\n\nPatient: The depression has definitely been there for the vast majority of these two years and five months, even when the paranoia and voices aren't as active. It has completely ruined my daily life and relationships because I've lost all my motivation to do basic tasks and have entirely withdrawn from everyone around me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been any periods where you experienced significant depressive symptoms without any psychotic symptoms, such as the feelings of being watched or hearing voices, and if so, how long did those periods last?\n\nPatient: Yes, absolutely. For the first year and a half of my struggles, I only had the severe depression without any of the paranoia or voices. Those scary experiences didn't start until about eight months ago.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your depressive symptoms changed, if at all, when the psychotic symptoms, such as the feelings of being watched and hearing voices, first appeared about eight months ago?\n\nPatient: When the paranoid feelings and voices started eight months ago, my depression became much more intense and exhausting. Dealing with the constant sadness alongside the terrifying fear of being watched made me feel completely overwhelmed and even more isolated.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of at least two weeks where you had no depressive symptoms, but still experienced psychotic symptoms such as delusions or hallucinations, which could help clarify the nature of your condition?\n\nPatient: Yes, there have been a few times over the last eight months where my depression lifted and my mood felt relatively normal, but the voices and the feeling of being watched remained just as strong for two or three weeks. It is incredibly exhausting because even when I'm not feeling down, the terror of being monitored never really stops."
  }

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type)", "Schizophrenia", "Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features"], "note": "The patient's symptoms of depression and psychotic experiences, such as delusions of being watched and hearing voices, have been present for a significant period. The presence of depressive symptoms for the majority of the two and a half years, with psychotic symptoms appearing later and sometimes occurring independently of the depressive episodes, suggests Schizoaffective Disorder (Depressive Type) as a strong candidate. However, the fact that the patient has experienced periods where depressive symptoms were absent but psychotic symptoms persisted could also support a diagnosis of Schizophrenia. Major Depressive Disorder with Psychotic Features remains a candidate due to the significant depressive symptoms and the presence of psychotic features, although the duration and independence of psychotic symptoms from depressive episodes make this less likely. Further evaluation is needed to determine the exact nature of the patient's condition.", "is_final": false}

For the first year and a half of my struggles, I only had the severe depression without any of the paranoia or voices. Those scary experiences didn't start until about eight months ago.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe how your depressive symptoms changed, if at all, when the psychotic symptoms, such as the feelings of being watched and hearing voices, first appeared about eight months ago?\n\nPatient: When the paranoid feelings and voices started eight months ago, my depression became much more intense and exhausting. Dealing with the constant sadness alongside the terrifying fear of being watched made me feel completely overwhelmed and even more isolated.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods of at least two weeks where you had no depressive symptoms, but still experienced psychotic symptoms such as delusions or hallucinations, which could help clarify the nature of your condition?\n\nPatient: Yes, there have been a few times over the last eight months where my depression lifted and my mood felt relatively normal, but the voices and the feeling of being watched remained just as strong for two or three weeks. It is incredibly exhausting because even when I'm not feeling down, the terror of being monitored never really stops.\n\nPatient: Yes, there have been a few times over the last eight months where my depression lifted and my mood felt relatively normal, but the voices and the feeling of being watched remained just as strong for two or three weeks.     "content": "/no_think\nYou are an assistant analyst connecting the [Symptom Profile] with the doctor's question.\nDetermine which part of the profile (chief complaint, associated symptoms, social history, etc.)\nthe doctor's **last question** is related to.\n\n[Symptom Profile]\n▸ psychotic_symptoms\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n  - Derailment or Loose Associations: A speech pattern of switching rapidly from one topic to another with no logical connection between thoughts.\n  - Tangentiality: Speech that deviates entirely from the question and fails to answer directly.\n  - Incoherence or Word Salad: Severe fragmentation of speech and sentence structure, making it nearly impossible to understand.\n  - Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  - Childlike Silliness: Inappropriate, childish, or illogical behavior appearing in situations.\n  - Stupor: No psychomotor activity; not actively relating to environment.\n  - Catalepsy: Passive induction of a posture held against gravity.\n  - Waxy Flexibility: Slight, even resistance to positioning by examiner.\n  - Mutism: No, or very little, verbal response despite the ability to speak.\n  - Negativism: Opposition or no response to instructions or external stimuli without apparent motive.\n  - Posturing: Spontaneous and active maintenance of a posture against gravity.\n  - Mannerism: Odd, circumstantial caricature of normal actions.\n  - Stereotypy: Repetitive, abnormally frequent, non-goal-directed movements.\n  - Agitation: Excessive motor activity without obvious external cause.\n  - Grimacing: Contorted facial expressions irrelevant to the situation.\n  - Echolalia: Mimicking another's speech.\n  - Echopraxia: Mimicking another's movements.\n  - Diminished Emotional Expression: A state of reduced emotional expression in facial expressions, eye contact, intonation of speech, and movements.\n  - Avolition: A decrease in motivated self-initiated purposeful activities.\n  - Alogia: A state where the quantity of spontaneous speech is significantly reduced.\n  - Anhedonia: A decrease in the ability to experience pleasure or interest in enjoyable activities.\n  - Asociality: A state where interest in social activities or interpersonal relationships is significantly reduced.\n  Duration: 8 months\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Loss of Interest: Reduced interest in previously enjoyed hobbies or leisure activities.\n  - Social Withdrawal: Reduced interest or effort in social interactions and maintaining relationships.\n  - Loss of Motivation: Lack of motivation to initiate or complete work or tasks.\n  - Anhedonia: Inability to experience positive emotions such as happiness, satisfaction, love, or pleasure.\n  - Indifference to Achievement: Insensitivity or indifference to personal achievements or success.\n  - Initial Insomnia: Difficulty falling asleep and taking a long time to do so.\n  - Middle Insomnia: Waking up frequently during sleep or having difficulty falling back asleep.\n  - Early Morning Awakening: Waking up too early and being unable to fall back asleep.\n  - Hypersomnia: Sleeping for long periods at night or excessive sleep during the day.\n  - Nightmare Disorder: Sleep disturbance caused by repeated and distressing dreams.\n  - Non-Restorative Sleep: Feeling unrefreshed despite having slept enough.\n  Duration: 6 months\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
